## 2.2 The Score as a Single Bilinear Product

Evaluated as written, (2) sums d elementwise terms for each of the NK pairs, which leaves idle the matrix-multipl<sub>y</sub> <sub>un</sub>it<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>supp</sub>l<sub>y mos</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> GPU’<sub>s</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>.</sub> N<sub>o</sub> lib<sub>rary</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>es</sub> it th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>way.</sub> I<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ea</sub>d th<sub>ey</sub> <sub>expan</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>qua</sub>d<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> f<sub>orm</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>assem</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>score</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>x</sub> <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> <sub>exac</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> <sub>as</sub> k-means expands $\| \boldsymbol { x } _ { i } - \boldsymbol { c } _ { k } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ t<sub>o reuse norms an</sub>d <sub>reac</sub>h th<sub>e</sub> same units (Johnson et al., 2021). We state the form we use below<sub>,</sub> since the fused kernels of §3 inherit it.

Lemma 1 (Lift). Let $\tau = 1 \oslash V \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K \times d }$ be the precisions, and define

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \tilde { X } = \left[ X \mid X \odot X \right] \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N \times 2 d } , } \\ & { \tilde { W } = \left[ M \odot \tau \mid - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \tau \right] \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K \times 2 d } , } \end{array}\tag{6}
$$

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \boldsymbol { b } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K } , \qquad b _ { k } = \log \pi _ { k } - \frac { d } 2 \log 2 \pi + \frac 1 2 \sum _ { c = 1 } ^ { d } \log \tau _ { k c } } } \\ & { - \frac 1 2 \sum _ { c = 1 } ^ { d } \mu _ { k c } ^ { 2 } \tau _ { k c } . } & { ( 7 ) } \end{array}
$$

Then the score matrix of (2) satisfies exactly, with $\mathbf { 1 } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ the all-ones column,

$$
S = \tilde { X } \tilde { W } ^ { \top } + \mathbf { 1 } b ^ { \top } .\tag{8}
$$

## 4 Exactness and Cost

W<sub>e es</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h th<sub>a</sub>t b<sub>o</sub>th f<sub>use</sub>d k<sub>erne</sub>l<sub>s compu</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d EM u<sub>p</sub>dates (Theorem 1) and characterize their HBM trafic (Theorem 2). Proofs are <sub>p</sub>rovided in A<sub>pp</sub>endix C.

Theorem 1 (Exactness). Fix N, $K \geq 1$ , data $X \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ ×d with $d \geq 1$ , weights $\pi > 0$ and variances $V > 0 ;$ take $B _ { K } \geq K$ in Algorithm 2, so that its single component tile is the whole axis, and assume padded lanes are predicated out on both axes, the statistic accumulators seeded at 0 and the fold of (10) seeded with any pair whose second coordinate is 0. Partition the rows $\{ 1 , \ldots , N \}$ into nonempty tiles, andfor Algorithm 3 partition the components $\{ 1 , \ldots , K \}$ likewise, each of its two passes choosing its partitions independently and pass 2 running after pass 1 has written $L ;$ tile sizes need divide neither N nor K. Then, in real arithmetic, Algorithms 2 and 3 return the statistics $n , \ s ,$ u of (4) and the log-likelihood ℓ of (1) at the incoming $( \pi , M , V ) , f o r$ every such partition and every order in which the tiles are visited. Moreover $n _ { k } > 0 f o r$ every k, so the updates (5) are defined and also agree.

It<sub>s con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo re</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>ons o</sub>f <sub>an</sub> it<sub>era</sub>ti<sub>on reasso-</sub> ciate inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub>: the statistics (4) fold over addition on the row axis, and the normalizer of (3) folds over the ⊕ of (10) on the com<sub>p</sub>onent axis. We state exactness a<sub>g</sub>ainst (1)– (4) rather than a<sub>g</sub>ainst Al<sub>g</sub>orithm 1, which is an archet<sub>yp</sub>e (§2.2) and so a weaker reference. The theorem also chains across the iterations of a run: each row of Γ sums to one, so $\textstyle \sum _ { k } n _ { k } = N$ <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s aga</sub>i<sub>n</sub> li<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e open</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>ex, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> $u _ { k } \oslash n _ { k } - \mu _ { k } \odot$ µ<sub>k</sub> is a weighted variance <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>ence nonnega</sub>ti<sub>ve, so</sub> $V _ { \mathrm { n e w } } \geq \varepsilon$ <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>one</sub> it<sub>era</sub>ti<sub>on sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>ypo</sub>th<sub>eses o</sub>f th<sub>e nex</sub>t <sub>w</sub>h<sub>enever</sub> $\varepsilon > 0$ (A<sub>pp</sub>endix C).

Theorem 2 (HBM trafic). Count elements moved between HBM and the chip, charging one for a read, one for a write, and twofor an atomic update, with no cache between the two; on-chip state is free. Let $T _ { N } = \lceil N / B _ { N } \rceil$ and $T _ { K } = \lceil K / B _ { K } \rceil$ be the tile counts. In $A l g o r i t h m \ 2$ the $T _ { N }$ row tiles are distributed over $P \leq T _ { N }$ programs, each loading the lift once and committing its partial statistics once; this is the launch our kernel uses and the only place the mapping enters a ledger. Then one iteration moves

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \underbrace { 5 N d + 6 N K + \Theta ( N + K d ) } _ { \mathrm { A l g o r i t h m 1 } } , } \\ & { \underbrace { N d + \Theta ( P K d ) } _ { \mathrm { A l g o r i t h m 2 } } , } \\ & { \underbrace { ( 1 + T _ { K } ) ( N d + N ) + \Theta ( T _ { N } K d ) } _ { \mathrm { A l g o r i t h m 3 } } } \end{array}\tag{9}
$$

elements, the parameter terms $\Theta ( P K d )$ and $\Theta ( T _ { N } K d )$ being upper bounds, since the lift is only $2 K d + K$ elements and may in practice stay resident in a last-level cache across row tiles. Whenever $P K = O ( N + K )$ , Algorithm 2 moves $\Theta ( N d + K d )$ , which is optimal up to constants among exact algorithms that receive X and return $( \pi _ { \mathrm { n e w } } , M _ { \mathrm { n e w } } , V _ { \mathrm { n e w } } )$ in HBM, while Algorithm 1 moves $\Theta ( N d + N K )$ whenever min $( K , d ) = O ( N )$ , in particular whenever $K \leq N$

Peak memory is flat in K, and at the widest setting it decides feasibility. Turbo-GMM never writes the $N \times$ K responsibilities, so its inputs govern its peak: from K = 128 to K = 4096 at $N = 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ t<sup>h</sup>at <sub>p</sub>ea<sup>k</sup> <sub>g</sub>rows onl<sub>y</sub> from 128 to 139 MB, a factor of 1.1, against 3.2 to 47 GB for the largest baseline peak with a valid fit— 25× to 338× ours, over the four librar<sub>y</sub> arms that report <sub>pea</sub>k <sub>memory</sub> <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>consequence</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>a</sub> <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>an</sub>t factor. At K = 4096, scikit-learn exhausts the 80 GB device and pomegranate fails outri<sub>g</sub>ht, while Turbo-GMM completes in 139 MB.

Against libraries, the advantage is ordered in d and disappears at small N. Measured against the fastest library with a valid fit at each shape, Turbo-GMM is 2.7–4.2× faster at large N and 2.5–2.6× at large K; taken one librar<sub>y</sub> at a time the maxima are 5.3× over P<sub>y</sub>Torch and 9.9× over scikit-learn. Ablation protocol, and two caveats. Their paper and their released code part on the row block. Their paper puts both sweeps inside one residency of the row block, which would read X once; the released diagonal kernel does not (§6). In flash gmm diag h100.py at commit 57c3621, two @triton.jit kernels are declared at lines 64 and 164, the row normalizer reaches HBM at line 130, and tl.load reads X at line 82 and again at line 233.
